# Fifteen days, mapped: the coverage of the Huang affair, who acted, and the documented ties

**Status: draft.** Every quoted passage has been matched by script to a saved copy of its source, and the counts in the
figure are recomputed from the tables here by `scripts/verify_nv8.py`. A tie is a dated record, not a motive. Nothing
here says why anyone acted.

![The figure](figures/nv8-fifteen-days-mapped.png)

Also as [HTML](figures/nv8-fifteen-days-mapped.html) (point at a dot to read the headline it stands for) and
[JPG](figures/nv8-fifteen-days-mapped.jpg). The image is large (two slices side by side, each with its own day axis): open it and zoom in. Since Nov 17 the PNG is taller than the JPEG format allows (no side over 65,500 pixels), so the JPG is the PNG scaled down to that height; the PNG is the full image.

## What this is

On Oct 29, 2026 The New York Times reported that U.S. officials saw Jensen Huang, the founder and then chief executive of
Nvidia, as the target of a Chinese spy plot. Fifteen days later he had resigned as chief executive and three House
committees had called him to answer questions. This figure puts three things on one sheet for those fifteen days:

- **The coverage.** One dot for each of the 2,245 pieces that two news aggregators, Techmeme and Memeorandum, listed
  on the story, on the first day a saved page lists it (2,254 pieces from 485 sites in all; 9 seen only on undated pages are
  not drawn).
- **Who acted.** One row for each actor (newspapers, reporters, analysts, 27 members of Congress, the administration,
  Nvidia, others) and one chip for each dated act: 422 acts, each worded from a saved report.
- **The documented ties.** At the left, the records that tie those actors to a company, a funder or a group: the AI
  company Anthropic, its people and its PAC; groups that Anthropic or the foundation Open Philanthropy has funded; and
  Nvidia itself. The ties are mapped outward from Anthropic and Open Philanthropy; Nvidia's are added where the same
  kind of record was searched, and the figure's note lists what was searched for it and what was not found. There are 150 lines for the 27 members of Congress (gifts by each company's
  employees, lobbying reports that name a member's bill, fellows and former staff, lobbyists' gifts, ads), a line on
  AnthroPAC's contribution for each member who received one, and 101 records among the other actors.

Why those parties: the matter touches the export of the AI chips that Nvidia sells. On the record here: Anthropic backs
"smart export controls on AI chips" and gave $40 million to the group Public First Action; members of Congress drawn
here sponsor bills on chip exports (one, the AI OVERWATCH Act, would let Congress block advanced chip exports); both
Anthropic's and Nvidia's lobbying reports name those bills; AMD and Broadcom compete with Nvidia for AI chip sales. The
figure's own boxes say, for each group of actors, what is on the record about it and what no document here shows.

A line ends at a person or a body, never at an act. The figure sets the records side by side; it does not say that any
of them caused a report, a post or a statement.

**The interactive map.** `docs/index.html` is the figure as a sheet to pan and zoom, with a panel that opens the record behind whatever is clicked: a chip's passages and links,
the record behind a tie line, every record at a box, a day's acts and headlines, everything that cites a source. GitHub Pages serves it; the link is in this repository's About box.
It is one static file built by `scripts/build_site_nv8.py` from the figure and the tables here, and holds nothing they do not. It is rebuilt after each change to them and says which commit it was built from.
The twelfth independent reading read its first public version: the records, dates, money and line ends agreed with the tables, and it found three errors in what the page showed around them, corrected since (below); the corrected page has been checked by script only.

Of the 150 lines for members of Congress, 54 are the two lines every member has, one on gifts by people who gave Anthropic as employer and one on Nvidia's (32 of the 54 say that none is on file). The other 96: 64 on gifts by lobbyists whose firms had clients among the groups drawn; 8 on contributions by the PAC of Americans for Responsible Innovation; 7 on lobbying reports that name the member's chip-export bill (both companies' counts); 6 on a Horizon fellow placed in an office or with a committee; 2 on a former adviser who sits on Anthropic's advisory council; 2 on testimony by Anthropic's people at a hearing; 2 on an ad by Public First Action naming the member; 2 on a former committee staff member now at a funded group; 1 on a former aide who lobbied for a funder; 1 on witnesses from groups Open Philanthropy has funded; 1 on a fellow of a funded group described as an adviser to a committee.
Each member drawn is named in at least three of the 1,163 articles counted, as separate reports: the 1,324 pages
saved for this project are 1,244 articles once copies of one article are joined, and a page dated outside the fifteen
days, a block or error page, or a page that is not a news piece is not counted (`research/story_pages.tsv` lists every page by address with its article,
whether it is counted and the members it names; `research/nv8_article_pairs.tsv` gives the verdict of the reader who opened each pair of pages that were
alike; `research/nv8_at_the_line.tsv` gives the reading of everyone at three or four articles).

## The figure's own notes

**Reading it:** the dots count pieces, not readers; a piece is placed on the first day a saved Techmeme or Memeorandum page lists it (pages for 9 am, noon, 4 pm, 8 pm and 11 pm Eastern each day), and 9 more pieces seen only on undated pages are left out. A piece counts as on the story when its address or listed headline names Huang, Megaspeed or Alice Huang, tells of the spy affair without naming them, or names Nvidia with a word of the affair (resign, board, inquiry, chief, successor and the like). That rule was applied by script, not by reading each piece, to the 2,465 links on those pages that name Huang, Nvidia, Megaspeed or the spy story. The 231 links the rule left out were then read, 150 of them by an independent auditor and the rest by the builder: 20 are plainly on the story and are counted with it, and 16 could not be settled from their headlines. Left out are 117 links on the wider fallout (export controls, the Commerce secretary, the share price, Anthropic's stock offering) and 94 others; a counted piece may name Huang for another reason. Every link, its class and each reading is published with the figure's tables. They are sorted into the three kinds by site and by the section named in the page address, not by reading them. A chip is one dated act, worded from a saved report; it starts on its day, and a long one may run on over empty days to its right. View counts are those shown when the posts were saved on Nov 12. An act is placed on the day its source gives: a day the source states, or one its context fixes (a remark dated with others like it, an answer to something that happened that day in a report of that day). Failing that it is placed on the day the source was first published: those chips have a dashed left edge, and the act itself may be earlier. For 20 of the acts so dated a cited page was changed after it was first published, and a passage taken from it (for some, one passage of several) may be newer than the act's day. Where one chip holds two acts of a day and only one is dated by its report, the edge is solid and the words say which. The rule for refusals to comment: an explicit refusal by a drawn actor about its own conduct or part in the matter is drawn once for each documented response, with the answer the same source prints beside it; refusals of one day to several outlets share an act that names each. The refusals found that way are listed with the tables; one that no reader's line records is not seen. A report that no reply had come to a request is not a refusal and is drawn for no one. A department's statements and refusals are drawn on its secretary's row. An act is drawn when a saved report gives it, it is on the matter and no chip of that actor already carries the same occurrence: a similar remark on another occasion is its own act; a later article repeating a dated remark is not. What is drawn was tested with three gauges over the readers' lines from the saved pages (an actor carried on a day with no chip; quoted remarks in two or more articles that no chip holds; the same words on two pages); they find candidates and do not prove completeness: an act reported in paraphrase, one that a single article carries on a day the actor already has a chip, or one under a speaker's name the gauges do not match to a drawn actor may still be missing. Where a chip rests on one outlet's account of another's report, it says so. Grant figures are awards, not payments traced to a bank. An announced ad is not proof that it ran. Nvidia's board said on Nov 8 it had "no reason to believe" national security or company secrets were compromised, and NBC reported on Nov 12 that Alice Huang's "precise role" remained unclear. Counts of who is named or quoted are counts of articles, each counted once: the 1,086 pages saved by the first search and the 238 saved since (the papers' own articles, saved by hand or in a licensed copy, a few transcripts and a company filing) are 1,244 articles once copies of one article are joined, and 1,163 of them are counted. Two pages are one article when they have the same address or when a reader judged them so: every two pages that share 25 or more seven-word runs making up a fifth of the shorter were opened by a reader from another model family (504 pairs over six readings; nine more were decided by the builder under a stated rule, and the table says which. Of the 513, 87 are the same article, as a syndicated or licensed copy with its lead kept, a later version at one address or two snapshots of a live page, and 426 are different, such as a statement and the articles quoting it, or a later dispatch with a new lead; four more could not be read, being block pages that are not counted). Copies that share less than that are not listed, so the number of articles can still be a little high. A page's day is the day of its first publication where the page shows one, and otherwise the day of the version saved, read as a U.S. Eastern day from the page's own stamp (51 pages whose metadata and text carry none, or carry one the printed stamp contradicts, were read in their saved copy). A page is not counted when that day is after Nov 12 (57 pages) or before Oct 29 (13, background pages), when the saved copy is a block or error page (6) or holds nothing at all (2), or when it is a reference page or an image (8). For 25 pages whose first extraction had missed the article, its text was taken again from the saved HTML (four of them a second time, after a reading found paragraphs missing or other lines among them); 100 counted pages are still held only in part (a headline and description, a teaser, an excerpt, the opening before a subscription barrier, a live page whose entries are collapsed or load only on request): 60 that the readers of the pages had listed, and 40 more found on Nov 16: 35 among the 248 counted pages that show a sign of a barrier or hold a short text, which readers went through, and five live pages that later readings found among the longer pages. A page is whole when all of the listed page's own piece is saved, whatever it quotes or links to. A longer page that shows no sign of a barrier was not read again for this: every counted page with a collapse mark in its text was read, and every saved page whose HTML holds a live-blog record was compared with its text; a live page saved by hand as PDF cannot be checked that way. On three live pages the saved HTML held 172 entries that the text read lacked, and they were added to it. One of them is a four-day live page of general news: an article is counted for a member when its text names the member anywhere, so there, as in any round-up, the name can stand in an entry about something else; the four members it names only for other news carry a note beside their count. On six pages a table or a photo caption of the piece, which the extraction from the saved HTML had left out, was added to the text read, after a search of every page's HTML for a member's name missing from its text (a member counts as named when the name stands in the piece's own table or caption), and on three pages a short paragraph or passage; other photo captions and some small parts of a piece (a subheading, a credit, a short sentence) are not in the text read, and that search does not find other passages an extraction may have lost. On four pages saved by hand as PDF, 21 lines of prose that the cleaning of the text had dropped at page breaks were put back. The ten people at three or four articles (eight members and two public voices) were each read for whether those articles are separate reports, and all have three. Two senators reach three only through a report saved on Nov 16 that the record had pointed to and the saved pages lacked: one through a piece the aggregators listed on Nov 12 (a paper's live page had repeated its own article, leaving two reports), one through a report that a reader had quoted under a blog post. The saved pages are about half of the pieces listed, so a member whose reports were not saved can be missing from these counts. Members of Congress drawn are every current member named in three or more of those 1,163 articles, as separate reports, for something said or done about the matter in these days, and one senator (Cotton) whom they name that often for a letter on Megaspeed a year earlier; each one's count is in their row. Ten others named that often are not drawn, because the pages name them for other things: a leadership vote, a lost election, a race still being counted, an impeachment bid, a missed vote and other inquiries. The names searched came from the readings of the pages, so on Nov 16 every sitting member's name was also searched as a whole name over the counted articles and over the headlines of the listed pieces that could not be saved (about half of the pieces listed were saved): 60 more members are named, none in more than two. The count for every name searched is published with the figure's tables. The articles counted quote 282 analysts, experts and research groups (a person or firm written two ways is counted once). Each was looked up in the records held. On Nov 15, and on Nov 16 and 17 for the names added since, the names not drawn were searched again as whole names in the 85 tables of the four evidence sets: 1,039 names of two to four words among the analysts and the bylines (people's names, and a few firms and labels of the same form), and 36 other firm names and one-word names among the analysts; 81 entries (bylines that are an agency, a label or an address, and analyst labels under four letters) were left out of that pass. The 16 drawn each have a remark of these days and a tie on record to Anthropic, Open Philanthropy or Nvidia: their own (a fellowship, research support, a past post, a seat on an advisory council) or that of the organisation where they hold a post (an award to it, a paid business relationship, a paper it was commissioned to write); each row says which, and an award to an organisation is not a payment to the person. At a university an award counts for a person only where the record names that person's own unit. Not drawn: 5 people at universities whose only award on record names the university as a whole, or another researcher there; 8 for whom the record is a remark or report not shown to be of these days, a past post at a group funded only later, a grant from another company, a grant to their group from an investor in Anthropic, or an invitation to speak with no post or payment on record; 2 the look-up could not tell apart from others of the same name; 17 entries that are one of the banks below or its analysts; and the other 234, for whom no record was found. No tie is implied for anyone not drawn. Bank analysts, and investors quoted for their view of the shares, are not drawn, whatever their ties; three investors, Ackman, Maguire and Conway, are drawn among the public voices, under that row's rule of three articles. Quartz reported on Sep 4, 2026, citing unnamed sources of Bloomberg's, that Anthropic was nearing a credit facility before its share sale, and named 17 banks in it, four of them also leading the sale; the report says the details could still change. Analysts, or executives in office, of 11 of those banks are quoted on the saved pages. The banks' business with Nvidia was not looked up. Two quoted investors are described on the pages as a shareholder and a short seller of Nvidia. The pages counted carry 966 bylines, counting a name once for each outlet it appears at: 888 different names, 75 of them agencies or not a person's name. These are the bylines of the pages counted, those of the first search and those saved since; a page saved as an actor's own original is cited on that actor's chip and its byline is not counted here. Each was looked up in the fellowship and staff records held and, on Nov 15, 16 or 17, in the whole-name search. Five have a tie on record and are drawn: Perlo and Hashim from the records held, Kubinec from his own bio at The Dispatch, Mowshowitz and Schneider from pages saved on Nov 15 (the first look-up had missed both). Two more have a record of another kind and no tie is drawn: a congressional fellow of the 2021 cohort of a programme that Open Philanthropy funded in 2025 (the record shows no overlap), and a reporter named on the page of a CSET event. A CSET staff member's byline on a CSET release of 2025 is no longer counted, because that page is dated before these days (he also wrote CSET's two releases of Nov 10 and 11, which are drawn in CSET's row). For four the tables hold the name and nothing ties it to the reporter. Pages that could not be saved were not checked. The lists of the names looked up are not published, because most name people for whom nothing was found; the counts and the rules are. "No AnthroPAC contribution on file" means none in the PAC's filings with the Federal Election Commission for Jun 29 to Sep 30, 2026. A line that says no gift from a company's employees means that no FEC line for Jan 2025 to Oct 14, 2026 gives that company as the donor's employer to a committee tied to the member; it is not proof that no employee gave. Lines dated after Oct 14, 2026 are not in the FEC records read here: where a committee must file a post-general report (its books close Nov 23), that report is due on Dec 3, 2026; other committees follow their own schedules. No line of a sitting member's campaign committee dated after Sep 30, 2026 is on file yet from either company's employees. Gifts made through the conduits ActBlue and WinRed after that day are on file only as the conduits' own lines (26 from people who gave Anthropic as employer, $2,741; 350 for Nvidia, $14,345) and are not counted until the recipient's own report shows them; among the members drawn they name only 2 Nvidia lines ($15) for the committee of Sen. Elizabeth Warren. A committee is tied to a member when it is the member's campaign committee, a leadership PAC the member sponsors, or a joint fundraising committee whose statement of organisation lists that member's committee and no other sitting member's; money given to a joint committee is shared with its other participants (a party committee, a PAC), and the FEC lines do not show how much reached the member's own campaign. Joint committees of several members are listed with the figure's tables and attributed to no one. An employee's gift is counted from an FEC line on which the donor gave the company as employer, to a committee tied to the member; donors are counted, not named, and the two companies are counted by one rule, though not from equally deep records, as the next sentences say. Both companies' lines come from the FEC's bulk file of itemised individual contributions for the 2026 cycle, downloaded Nov 13, 2026 (it carries whole dollars), plus the lines its API holds and the file does not yet (nearly all dated Jul to Sep 2026): 80 for Anthropic, 21 for Nvidia. For 8 more lines the API's later filing replaces the file's copy. Nine Anthropic lines to Banks for Senate are counted from a copy of the committee's report saved in Sep 2026: its amended report leaves the employer blank. No earlier copy exists for Nvidia, so a Nvidia line blanked that way is missed. Sums are of positive receipt lines, in whole dollars as the FEC's bulk file gives them. Negative lines on the same committees (refunds and chargebacks: 3 for Anthropic, $4,287; 4 for Nvidia, $200) are listed with the tables and not taken off; among the members drawn, $3,500 returned by a committee of Sen. John Cornyn. A person is counted by surname and first given name; in six of the counts a name occurs with a second spelling or ZIP code, which shows neither two people nor one, and the count stays as the rule gives it. A lobbying report names a bill; it does not say which office was visited or what was asked. Fellows, advisers and former staff are as the programme's, the group's or Congress's own pages list them. A lobbyists' line names the clients that the lobbying reports held here list for the givers' firms. The reports give each gift as the lobbyist's own money; they do not show whether a client asked for it. Whether the firm had the client when the gift was made is taken from the firm's registration dates, which the published table gives beside each gift. Why each client is on this map: Open Philanthropy's awards on record include $3.4M to Americans for Responsible Innovation (2024–25), $12.5M to the Center for AI Safety (2022–23) and $1.9M to the Future of Life Institute (2015–20), and its action fund gave IFP Action $2.0M (2022–23). The grants on record to the Center for AI Safety's action fund and to Encode AI are from Jaan Tallinn, and the Future of Life Institute gave Encode $250,000 in 2024; no Open Philanthropy award to either is on record, and none of any kind to Fathom. Nvidia registered its own political committee on Aug 27, 2026; its one report on file, through Sep 30, shows no contribution to any candidate. The same record was then pulled for both companies: the lobbyists' contribution reports for 2020 to 2026 of all 33 firms registered for Nvidia, for Anthropic or for one of the groups above. To the 27 members drawn, in gifts of Feb 2021 to Jun 2026 made while the firm was registered for the client, lobbyists listed for the client gave $70,469 on Nvidia's side, $78,500 on Anthropic's and $85,491 on the groups' side; counting every lobbyist at those firms, $429,735, $124,500 and $295,629. A firm registered for two clients puts one gift on two sides, so the sides are not added up. Each member's row gives its lines from this table by the same rule for each side: every gift of a lobbyist at a firm then registered for the client, and how much of it came from lobbyists listed for that client. Only a lobbyist's own money is counted. The reports also list contributions of political committees (a firm's PAC, a PAC a lobbyist serves, a campaign committee): 55 such lines, 28 gifts once those listed by several lobbyists of one firm are counted once, are in the table and in no sum here; until Nov 16, 2026 they were counted as lobbyists' own money. The table is published with the figure. For the Speaker and for Sen. Kennedy, who share a surname with other members, only a gift whose report gives the office is counted; for Sen. Scott, whose surname several other politicians carry, only one whose report gives his given name or its initial.

**What the record does not show:** who gave the intelligence assessment to reporters; whether it is correct; any contact about these reports between Anthropic, or anyone tied to it, and the reporters or their sources; any contact with a member of Congress about these reports or about what the member then said or did (earlier public contacts, such as testimony at a hearing, are drawn at left). The saved reports state limits of their own: the Times's first report says officials "did not believe" Huang knew the woman was gathering information when the president was first briefed, and that Huang "has not received a defensive briefing"; Nvidia said its customers get "zero information relevant to national security", and the Times reports its spokesman saying that customers do not ask Nvidia to press the U.S. government to change its policies on chip sales to China. That first report also says it "could not be determined which intelligence agency or agencies" monitored her, and that it is not clear what information she passed along or what it was worth to China. NBC reports that the president, asked whether he was briefed before he took Huang to China in May, said "I didn't hear about it then" and later "They could have been mentioned."

**Nvidia's side, the same classes of record:** its employees' gifts are drawn beside Anthropic's; its political committee is new and has given to no candidate; its lobbyists' gifts are counted above with Anthropic's and the groups'; bills named in its lobbying reports are drawn; past posts with a drawn member that its lobbyists declare are drawn (Banks, Kennedy, McCaul), as is the one found for a lobbyist of Anthropic (Thune). Its support of institutions whose people are drawn is worded in their rows (CSET, CNAS, the Council on Foreign Relations); the donor lists of Brookings and 80,000 Hours do not name it, and a reader from another model family found it on none of the lists that IAPS, the Foundation for American Innovation, the Institute for Progress and the Tarbell Center publish, most of which are partial. Quoted people with a past post at Nvidia or paid work for it are drawn (Moorhead, Newman, Boloor, Yoo; both companies support Barez's research), and business with Nvidia that a saved page states is worded for two public voices (Musk, Delangue). Looked for and not established: a witness from Nvidia before these members' committees, advertising aimed at their districts, a seat on their advisory bodies. Those searches have gaps: the ad libraries could not be read, the search of the records held ended with 3,839 hits unread, and the web search left 491 targets unopened. The map still starts from Anthropic and Open Philanthropy at the left of each slice, so the number of lines says nothing about either company's influence. The Times's and the FT's reports are cited from their own articles, saved by hand; the Journal's own site turns away automated readers: its report of Oct 30 is cited from its own page, saved by hand, and from the copy a licensed partner carries (Tiger Brokers), its report of Nov 11 from a partner's copy (the Greek paper To Vima). An earlier report that a paper attributed to an Anthropic executive is drawn as a tie in that paper's row: one is on record, the FT's of Sep 2025, known here through another outlet's account of it, which does not say whether the FT alone had it. The papers' earlier reporting was not searched for others, and reports attributed to Nvidia executives were not looked up. The own channel (official site, newsroom, own feed or account) of each of the 82 actors who had spoken was searched once for these fifteen days by a reader from another model family: on Nov 14 for the 63 then drawn, on Nov 15 for the 14 added since and then for the three members and the two others drawn later that day (the two share-price rows have none; one voice among the 82 is no longer drawn). An item about the affair is held and was read for 41 (the three papers among them, through articles saved by hand); for two links were located and none could be saved (Anthropic's SEC filings, a page of OpenAI's); four had only items that do not mention the affair or are not their own; for 25 a channel that could be read showed no item about the affair in these fifteen days; for 10 no channel could be read. An item is counted only where the project holds the page, and a share-sale filing or policy paper that a chip draws as context is not counted as an item about the affair. The reader could open no account on X in either round, and a channel with nothing found is not proof of silence. Of the 133 chips drawn before those searches, the original was read and is now cited for 36; for seven more the search did not yield the original, but a saved copy of the publisher's page is now cited (one of them shows only the opening); for nine more the reader could not open a post on X, and the post itself, saved afterwards by its id, is now cited; for one it carries the first of the chip's two remarks; for 71 it still could not be opened (posts on X and Truth Social, broadcasters' video, papers behind a paywall); for two the reader found it and the site refused the project's own copy (the Journal's page, an SEC filing); and for 7 it was not found. Of the chips still without their original, 14 now also cite a paper's own report of the act, from the papers' pages saved since the first census. The 290 acts added without such a search (two acts of one day share a chip; the two acts added and later withdrawn as duplicates are not counted) each cite the actor's own page, the paper's own article (some in a licensed copy) or, where neither is held, a report that the chip names.

**Source:** 2,254 links from saved aggregator pages; saved reports, posts and releases; committee filings; rows held in the congress, lineage, toner and evaluators packs. Aggregator pages for Oct 29 to Nov 12 were retrieved on Nov 12 and Nov 14, 2026; other sources were saved Nov 12 to 15, 2026; rows of the packs carry their own earlier dates.

**Status:** draft of Nov 16, 2026. Each passage behind a drawn item was matched by script to a saved copy of its source. Thirty-one independent readings by another company's model have read the figure or the corrections made to it. The first (Nov 14) read the 230 rows then drawn that are not listed pieces, recounted the pieces and recomputed the members' lines. The second (Nov 15), in three parts, read the 168 rows new or changed since then, the figure element by element, the pieces and the money recounted from the FEC's saved records, and who is drawn and what the chips carry; it graded 11 points as errors and 145 as needing a qualification. The third (Nov 15) read the corrections made in answer: it found no error in the 90 changed rows, two errors and eight qualifications in the changed text, and held 2 of the 181 answers insufficient and 19 only in part. A fourth (Nov 15) read the answers to it and the 45 rows added or changed since: no error in a row, 24 qualifications (18 of them one point, that lobbyists' gifts were shown by unequal measures), two errors and nine qualifications in the changed text, and 13 of 51 answers held only in part. A fifth (Nov 15) read the answers to that: no problem in the 44 rows it read, four points on the wording of the notes, and 48 of 49 answers held. The most serious points across the first six: a PAC gift that was recorded and not drawn; joint committees tied to a member before their statements were read; look-ups that had missed records the tables hold, three banks among them; chips that left out a clause qualifying what was said; and people left out by a rule that was not applied alike to both companies. Each finding has a written disposition, published with the tables. No reading fetched the originals again. A sixth (Nov 15) graded the answers to the fifth and found them right but for one sentence of the notes, which now stands in the wording that reading proposed. A seventh (Nov 15) read the 67 rows added or changed after the sixth and the new counting of articles: 62 rows ok, five needing a qualification, none in error; it found the counting of articles unsound (10 of 25 joined pairs it sampled were different works, four of 15 unjoined pairs the same; dates read from headers a day off; one senator drawn on three articles that are two reports). An eighth (Nov 16) read the answers to that: the ten changed rows ok and 59 of 66 answers holding, but the count of articles still not reliable (wire copies not joined, five articles left out although their saved pages hold them, two empty pages counted), and summaries that still counted a senator withdrawn in that release. In answer a third reader opened every pair of pages that share a fifth of their text, the article text of 25 pages was taken again from the saved pages, that senator (Scott) is drawn again and another (Warner) no longer rests on a reader's comment, each on a third report saved since, and 54 more pages saved by hand on Nov 16 were read and counted. A ninth (Nov 16) read both of those rounds: nine of the ten changed rows ok and 63 of 72 answers holding; one member's line counted a political committee's contribution as a lobbyist's own money, a company filing's event date had been taken for its day of publication, and three of the re-extracted pages still lacked a paragraph. In answer the fault in the lobbyists' lines was looked for in every member's and found in 54 lines, the chips on the resignation and the appointments were given the filing's dates, and four pages were taken again and read again. A tenth (Nov 16) read those answers: the 17 changed rows ok and all 54 answers holding, 12 of them in part; one line of committee money had still been missed (a campaign committee whose name holds the lobbyist's surname), one representative's count of articles was two short, and two sentences claimed too much (how many headlines name people who are not drawn; that an independent reader judged every pair of pages). In answer a contribution counts as a lobbyist's own only when the report says SELF or gives the filer's name and nothing else, which corrected one more member's line (the groups' side is $295,629; it was $391,629 before the ninth reading), and that representative is counted under his legal given name too. An eleventh (Nov 16) read those answers and the same corrections in the three-chairmen figure: all 46 answers hold, and it found no misclassified line of money among the 193 of 678 lines it read, the rule run on all 678 agreeing. It found one counted page that is a subscription preview and was not marked as held in part, a sentence in a supporting table saying that no client directed the lobbyists' gifts, which the reports do not show, the names searched for some members lacking a form the roster gives, and this note's count of readings out of date. In answer a reader went through the 248 counted pages that show a sign of a barrier or hold a short text, which raised the pages marked as held only in part from 58 to 92; a table and five photo captions that the extraction had left out were added to the text read, which raised four members' counts of articles (Mast 91, Moolenaar 78, Crawford 68, the Speaker 10); the given name or its usual short form was added to the names searched for eleven members, changing no count; and the supporting table's sentence was replaced. A twelfth (Nov 16) read those answers and the interactive map made from the figure: every member's count, the added passages and the money reproduce, and 37 of 40 answers hold, three in part. It found, on the interactive map, members' lines shown without their sources, searches that found nothing counted as ties, and a label claiming a current post; and in the tables a complete newsletter marked as held in part while one cut off by its paywall was not, a live page with collapsed entries not marked, lines of prose dropped at the page breaks of a page saved by hand as PDF, and photo captions missing from the text read. It closed incomplete: four hand-saved PDFs could not be opened where it ran. In answer the interactive map lists every source, sets the empty searches apart and is relabelled; 93 pages were marked as held only in part, under the stated rule that a page is whole when all of its own piece is saved; 21 dropped lines were put back on four pages and two pages trimmed of a following article's opening lines; and the missing captions were stated as a limit. A thirteenth (Nov 16) read those answers and opened the four PDFs: 72 of 83 answers hold and 11 hold in part; every restored line and both trims are right, and the stated limit on captions is enough for the counts. It found a second newsletter that stops at its own paywall and was not marked; three things about live pages (one whose saved HTML holds 143 entries, of which the text read held 21; one that holds 69, of which 20 were read; and four copies of one live page that hold only its latest entries and were not marked); the three-chairmen figure counting two posts from a text since replaced; and the search for collapse marks described as wider than it was. It closed incomplete: two source views were not available to it. In answer 172 entries were added to the text read of three live pages, which raised eight members' counts by one or two (Warren 71, Coons 48, Jeffries 30, Schumer 22, Thune 13, Johnson 11, Cornyn 9, Mast 92); 98 pages were marked as held only in part; every counted page with any collapse mark was read, and every saved page with a live-blog record was compared with its text; a census reader read the three live pages again for remarks; and the three-chairmen figure was made to read the same text as this one (93, 81 and 74 pages). A fourteenth (Nov 16) read those answers: every count reproduces, 36 of 43 answers hold and seven hold in part, and it would keep the rule that counts a live page of general news like any article, with the four members it names only for other news marked more plainly. It found two acts in the new census reading that no chip carried (a representative's remark of Nov 12 and a reported Justice Department inquiry), a letter's signature and two summary bullets still missing from two live pages, and four sentences that said too much or dated a search to one day. It closed incomplete: whether one live page saved as PDF had older entries cannot be settled from the PDF. Changed in answer and not yet read independently: the two acts are drawn, and a second gauge over every drawn actor and day found six more acts that two or more articles report in different words and no chip carried (among them the attorney general's statement of Nov 12 that the department had reached no conclusions about the woman in the reports); eight acts are added in all; one older act moved from its report's day to its own, and one chip's dashed edge became solid. The four members are marked beside their counts; the small parts are added; the sentences are corrected. After that release the builder read what its own gauges had left unread (the 31 days on which only one counted article carries an actor who has no chip that day; and, with a third gauge, quoted remarks that two or more articles carry in differing words) and drew thirteen more acts, among them the president's answer of Nov 12 that it was not yet determined whether the woman was a spy, and Nvidia's and the Chinese embassy's replies to the Times report of Nov 6. A fifteenth (Nov 16) read the release before those additions: every count reproduces; among 18 of those 31 days it found eight acts with no chip, six of which the builder had drawn by then; it found one chip that put a reply in the wrong room, one that left out its source's statement that Huang is not a target of the inquiry it reports, one that did not name the outlet relaying a broadcast, two wrong count labels, and seven dashed edges it would make solid; it noted that Anthropic's refusal to comment was drawn while other parties' refusals were not. It closed complete, with the earlier open question carried. In answer 22 acts were added to those of the fourteenth reading's answer (194 dated acts), among them three the reading found beside reasons the builder had given for drawing nothing (a company clarification, a post Elon Musk agreed with, a senator's statement of Nov 12); thirteen edges were made solid, the day being read from a source's ordinary context; the three chips and the two labels were corrected; a rule for refusals to comment was written; and a note was added on chips whose page was changed after its first day. A sixteenth (Nov 16) read those answers: 37 of the 39 new or changed rows hold, 54 of its 56 recounts agree, all 27 members' counts among them, and 39 of 44 answers hold, five in part. It found that the rule for refusals was not applied alike: six refusals that met it were not drawn (two each of Nvidia's, the White House's and the Justice Department's) and one was drawn outside it. It also found a Commerce chip without the answer its source prints beside the refusal; two readings in which the builder had merged a separate act into an existing chip (a chairman's note to employees, a think tank's piece); four acts with no chip among 30 actor-days it sampled, each reported in paraphrase or merged; and three chips on pages updated after their first day that the screen had missed. It closed complete, with the earlier open question carried. Changed in answer and not yet read independently: the rule for refusals is now the one the reading proposed (every explicit refusal by a drawn actor about its own part, once for each documented response, with any answer printed beside it), which adds twelve acts; eight more acts are drawn (two reported retainers of Huang's, a Commerce rule in preparation and a rule pause, the chairman's note, the think tank's piece, the interim chief's meeting, a senator's re-issued warning); the Commerce chip carries its answer; and the screen reads the update lines that pages print. The builder then searched every line of the census readings for words of refusal, beyond the eight parties the reading had listed, and drew what it found: Singapore's police, five acts of Taiwan's government (its row, until now its interior minister's, is the government's) and the president, whose answer of Nov 9 about what he had been told ("it didn't make much of an impact on me") eighteen saved pages carry and no chip did. That search showed two faults in the gauges, which the builder then worked through. The gauges matched a line of the census readings to an actor by a list of names that lacked 148 labels (an official named in person, "the FBI", "China"): with them added, 407 more lines were read, from which the builder drew one act and additions to two chips. And the third gauge had left out quoted remarks that an earlier judgment called part of an occasion already drawn: the builder read all 102 such groups against their chips and added twelve acts for parts that no chip carried. It says the omissions run in several directions and do not establish a preference for Anthropic. It closed complete, with the earlier open question carried. Changed in answer: fifteen acts added and twelve chips amended as that reading specified; one refusal moved a day earlier, to the first saved report of it, with the outlet that first reported it named; the labels added; and the table of refusals rebuilt from the published sources, with every outside line classified. It says its omissions run in no single direction, that none concerns Anthropic, and that the four tests do not lead two careful readers to the same list. It closed complete, with the earlier open question carried. Beside it, three other readers of that model read what the gauges do not see, each with acts held out of its table as controls (ten of eleven were called missing; the eleventh, a report of these days about a call of 2025, was set aside as the brief told the reader): the 294 quoted remarks of drawn actors that one article gives and no chip carried, and the 2,027 paraphrased lines of Nvidia, its board, its chairman and its interim chief that no chip carried by its words. They proposed 142 additions, by their own coding more of them for Huang or Nvidia than against; the builder checked each against its saved page and drew 125, the other 17 are listed with the reason. With the eighteenth reading's points that makes 41 acts added and 36 act rows amended. Then six more such readers took the paraphrased lines of every other drawn actor (2,949 lines; twelve acts held out, eleven called missing and one judged carried by another row's chip). Beyond the controls they proposed 108 additions: 55 are drawn, on 20 new acts and 23 amended chips, and 53 are listed with the reason (most are a paper's report that the paper's own row carries, a page's summary that names no occasion, or a relay). Four of the six closed incomplete: 250 lines have no verdict, most of them relays of a denial that could not be tied to one drawn response rather than another. The second stays on Oct 31, for another reason than the one given before: a licensed copy of an earlier version of the article, timed Oct 30, does not hold the sentence, so the chip sits on the day of the earliest saved version that does, and says so; that copy does hold the Chinese embassy's reply, which moves to Oct 30, and the Taiwan office's. Three of the ten occasions and four of the eleven parts were already drawn from the six lanes; the rest are drawn now. In all, five acts are added and fifteen act rows amended. One of the 28 acts and one earlier day come from the builder's own search of the saved pages for other wordings of each recall. That search read phrases, not pages. It found fourteen reported occasions on no chip and four further parts of statements, and that what the builder had left out of the set-aside items leans toward material favourable to Huang and Nvidia (six of eleven exclusions, none against them). All 69 earlier answers hold, one in part, and the interactive map matches the tables. Six further readers sorted them into groups, one for each earlier act as far as identity could be proved: with twenty-seven acts the builder has added since, and 195 that five more readers formed from the earlier passages on pages another session saved (44 of them summaries in which their reader found no occasion, kept as unresolved records since the twenty-ninth reading), there are 994, of which 117 are carried by a chip or a tie line and 877 are counted as open: 846 on no chip and thirty-one that a chip or a tie line words in part (1,631 candidate lines on 676 pages: 1,101 on 527 counted pages and 530 on 149 pages another session saved, which the table of counted pages does not hold; the groups' own line counts sum to 1,633, because one of their lines is a planted control and one is a line whose reader had named a chip that does not carry the act). Of the 877, the pages present 61 as first reported in these days and 266 as public before, and for 550 they do not say; 388 are statements, 243 events and 154 decisions. By the readers' coding (the builder's for the acts it added) 330 cut against Huang or Nvidia, 214 for them, 48 against the administration, 46 for it, and 239 neither way. It also showed one open line that concerns Anthropic, now on the tie line between Anthropic and Nvidia; a search of every line for Anthropic's name found one more, a review of Huang's podcast remarks that its publisher scored with AI models of Anthropic and OpenAI, and the chip that relays the review now says so. The twenty-second reading (Nov 17) read those answers, the lists and the register, and closed `audit: incomplete whole-statement continuation and primary-source/version follow-through`: it did not read the fourteen further whole statements asked of it, read long cited articles at their passages and not from end to end, and did not follow every page of the screen's longer lists. Of 28 new or changed rows it found 23 right, four in need of a qualification and one wrong: the recalled filing, moved to Nov 8 in the last answer, is recalled a day earlier in the Times's report on licences, and a relay of that day proves the sentences were in it. It found four earlier acts that no chip and no line of the lists held (a letter of Senate Democrats that the FT recalls, two investments by Nvidia and its financing agreements of August), three groups marked carried whose chip did not word the act, and that the number of open groups is no limit on the number of distinct acts, since some groups hold several occasions. Changed in answer and not yet read independently: those four acts and a fifth (Nvidia's investment in OpenAI) are in the register as open, with 31 lines the builder added; one group's chip now words its act (the Justice Department's request that Commerce pause, in the Times's own sentence) and two groups are open again; and three chips and the two background lines on Anthropic's and OpenAI's rows gain what their sources print (a caution that an employees' statement is no proof of a corporate campaign, two denials, a note that a sentence may be newer, a relay's account that the president was notified). The twenty-third reading (Nov 17) read that answer and closed `audit: incomplete fourteen whole statements not completed`: of nine new or changed rows it found seven right and two to qualify, and eleven distinct problems, none of them an occasion missing from both the figure and the register. It found that one group the register counted as open was carried by the very chip that answer had added (a forecast of August that assumed no sales to China), so that the sentence then printed here, that none of the open groups is drawn, was false; that a relay of Nov 7 had been put on a chip of Nov 6; and that a wire sentence moved to the new chip had lost the words that it may be newer than its page's first day. Changed in answer and not yet read independently: the relay has its own act on the FT's row at Nov 7; the caveat is back; the group is marked carried; two acts are registered as open (a blog's report that the secretary of state said he had shared the information, of which no original is saved; and a withheld package of additions to the trade blacklist, from the pages described next); two census lines, two kinds and the notes of three investments are corrected.
